# Weekly Build Activity Journal

## CS 590 Low Code No Code for Rapid Prototyping

**Student Name:** Connor Macejak  
**Week:** 6  
**Date Range:** 9/28/26 to 10/4/26  
**List Course Work and/or Project:** Week 6 News Aggregator Activity and Sprint 2 - GitHub Issue Triage Automation

Use this journal to give an accurate weekly account of your work. Specific evidence and an honest diagnosis matter more than a smooth success story. A failed build, a blocked stack, or unfinished work can earn full credit when you show what happened, what you tried, and what you will do next.

## 1\. Weekly Focus and Goals \[1 point\]

State the current sprint, lab, or project objective. Then list up to three specific goals you planned to complete this week.

- Goal 1: Complete the Week 6 Personal Tech-News Aggregator activity in n8n.
- Goal 2: Set up the Sprint 2 GitHub Issue Triage workflow and persistent data tables.
- Goal 3: Build the issue classification, duplicate-safety, and routing logic for Sprint 2.

## 2\. Completed Work and Evidence \[3 points\]

For each completed item, state what you changed or built and attach or link the evidence. Evidence may be a screenshot, repository commit, Figma file, Baserow base, n8n workflow export, app link, test output, or another inspectable artifact.

## 4\. What You Learned \[2 points\]

Describe the result of this week's work. Include one decision, observation, or result that changed your understanding of the problem, user flow, data model, workflow, or platform.

Then name the prototype dimension you tested most directly this week, **role**, **look and feel**, **implementation**, **data shape**, or **process**, and one important question your work did not answer.

This week, I learned that a successful workflow execution does not always mean the workflow is completely correct. The Week 6 activity showed the importance of checking the data that is actually saved, while Sprint 2 showed how duplicate safety and routing logic need to be designed before testing real GitHub events. Building the classification rules also helped me understand how a workflow can make predictable routing decisions based on issue titles instead of relying on manual decisions.

The prototype dimension I tested most directly this week was process, because I focused on how data moves through the workflow from input, to classification, to duplicate checking, and then to routing. One question that is still unanswered is whether the workflow will correctly handle all of the required GitHub issue tests and webhook redeliveries without creating duplicate records.

##

## 5\. Challenge and Diagnosis \[2 points\]

Describe one obstacle honestly and specifically.

- **What happened?** Include the exact error, unexpected behavior, unclear requirement, or missed plan.
- **What did you try or rule out?**
- **What will you try next?**

One challenge this week was setting up the classification and routing logic correctly in Sprint 2. The workflow needed to identify different words in a GitHub issue title and route the issue to the correct category, while also keeping the duplicate-safety logic connected properly. I reviewed the required Switch rules and checked the node connections to make sure each category would follow the correct route. Since I have not completed the Part 4 issue testing yet, the next step is to create the required GitHub test issues and verify that each category is classified and routed correctly without creating duplicate records.

State what you anticipate to address next week. If you need instructor help, please send an email!

Next week, I plan to continue Sprint 2 by completing the required GitHub issue tests, verifying the classification and routing results, and checking that duplicate webhook deliveries do not create duplicate records. I will also finish any remaining documentation and evidence needed for the Sprint 2 submission.
